Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There are $n$ dormitories in Berland State University, they are numbered with integers from $1$ to $n$. Each dormitory consists of rooms, there are $a_i$ rooms in $i$-th dormitory. The rooms in $i$-th dormitory are numbered from $1$ to $a_i$.

A postman delivers letters. Sometimes there is no specific dormitory and room number in it on an envelope. Instead of it only a room number among all rooms of all $n$ dormitories is written on an envelope. In this case, assume that all the rooms are numbered from $1$ to $a_1 + a_2 + \dots + a_n$ and the rooms of the first dormitory go first, the rooms of the second dormitory go after them and so on.

For example, in case $n=2$, $a_1=3$ and $a_2=5$ an envelope can have any integer from $1$ to $8$ written on it. If the number $7$ is written on an envelope, it means that the letter should be delivered to the room number $4$ of the second dormitory.

For each of $m$ letters by the room number among all $n$ dormitories, determine the particular dormitory and the room number in a dormitory where this letter should be delivered.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers $n$ and $m$ $(1 \le n, m \le 2 \cdot 10^{5})$ — the number of dormitories and the number of letters.

The second line contains a sequence $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ $(1 \le a_i \le 10^{10})$, where $a_i$ equals to the number of rooms in the $i$-th dormitory. The third line contains a sequence $b_1, b_2, \dots, b_m$ $(1 \le b_j \le a_1 + a_2 + \dots + a_n)$, where $b_j$ equals to the room number (among all rooms of all dormitories) for the $j$-th letter. All $b_j$ are given in increasing order.


-----Output-----

Print $m$ lines. For each letter print two integers $f$ and $k$ — the dormitory number $f$ $(1 \le f \le n)$ and the room number $k$ in this dormitory $(1 \le k \le a_f)$ to deliver the letter.


-----Examples-----
Input
3 6
10 15 12
1 9 12 23 26 37

Output
1 1
1 9
2 2
2 13
3 1
3 12

Input
2 3
5 10000000000
5 6 9999999999

Output
1 5
2 1
2 9999999994



-----Note-----

In the first example letters should be delivered in the following order:  the first letter in room $1$ of the first dormitory  the second letter in room $9$ of the first dormitory  the third letter in room $2$ of the second dormitory  the fourth letter in room $13$ of the second dormitory  the fifth letter in room $1$ of the third dormitory  the sixth letter in room $12$ of the third dormitory

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` dormitories, each with `a_i` rooms
2. Rooms are numbered sequentially across all dormitories: first all rooms of dorm 1, then dorm 2, etc.
3. For each letter with a room number among all rooms, we need to find which dormitory and which room within that dormitory it belongs to
4. The input gives us the total room numbers in sorted order

## Key Insights

1. We need to find which dormitory contains a given room number by checking the cumulative sum of rooms
2. For a given room number `b_j`, we need to find the first dormitory `f` such that the cumulative sum of rooms from dorm 1 to dorm `f` is >= `b_j`
3. Once we find the dormitory, the room number within that dormitory is `b_j - (sum of rooms in previous dormitories)`

## Algorithm Steps

1. Calculate prefix sums of the dormitory room counts
2. For each query room number:
   - Find the first dormitory where the cumulative sum >= query room number
   - The room number within that dormitory = query room number - cumulative sum of all previous dormitories

## Implementation Approach

Since we have up to 200,000 queries and dormitories, we can't do a linear search for each query. Instead, we can precompute the prefix sums and use binary search to find the appropriate dormitory for each query.

However, given that the queries are already sorted, we can use binary search or even iterate through dormitories efficiently.

But note: we have many queries, so we should use binary search for efficiency.

Wait, actually we can also use a two-pointer approach since queries are sorted. But the most straightforward solution is to precompute prefix sums and then for each query, binary search for the dormitory.

Alternatively, since the queries are sorted, we can iterate through the dormitories once and handle all queries that fall in the same dormitory range.

But for simplicity and clarity, I'll use binary search for each query since `m` and `n` are up to 200,000, and binary search will be O(m * log n) which is acceptable.

Actually, we can do better: since queries are sorted, we can use a single forward pass through dormitories and for each dormitory, process all queries that fall in that dormitory's range.

But for simplicity and correctness, I'll first implement the binary search approach.

Wait, actually let's think again: we have `m` queries and `n` dormitories. We can precompute prefix sums, then for each query, binary search to find which dormitory it belongs to.

But since we have up to 200,000 queries, and binary search per query would be O(m * log n) which is about 200,000 * log2(200,000) ≈ 200,000 * 18 = 3.6 million operations, which is acceptable in Python.

Alternatively, we can use a two-pointer approach: since queries are sorted, we can iterate through dormitories and for each dormitory, find the queries that fall in its range.

Let me implement the binary search approach for clarity.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read n, m
2. Read array a of dormitory room counts
3. Precompute prefix sums: prefix[i] = a[0] + a[1] + ... + a[i-1]
4. For each query b_j:
   - Use binary search to find the smallest index i (0-indexed) such that prefix[i+1] >= b_j
   - Dormitory number = i+1 (1-indexed)
   - Room number = b_j - prefix[i]

Actually, let me verify the prefix sum:
- prefix[0] = 0
- prefix[1] = a[0]
- prefix[2] = a[0] + a[1]
- ...

So if we have a room number b, we want to find the smallest i such that prefix[i] >= b, then it's in dormitory i (1-indexed) and room number is b - prefix[i-1].

Wait, let me correct:
- Dormitory 1: rooms 1 to prefix[1] = a[0]
- Dormitory 2: rooms prefix[1]+1 to prefix[2] = a[0]+a[1]
- Dormitory i: rooms prefix[i-1]+1 to prefix[i]

So for a room number b, we want the smallest i such that prefix[i] >= b.
Then the dormitory is i (1-indexed), and room number is b - prefix[i-1].

Yes.

So algorithm:
1. Precompute prefix array of length n+1 where prefix[0]=0, prefix[i]=sum of first i elements of a
2. For each query b:
   - Find leftmost index i in range [0, n] such that prefix[i] >= b
   - Dormitory = i (since i ranges from 1 to n, when i=1, prefix[1]>=b means b <= a[0], so dorm 1)
   - Actually when i=0: prefix[0]=0, if b>0, we won't have prefix[0]>=b
   - So we find the smallest i where prefix[i] >= b, then dormitory is i (1-indexed)
   - But i ranges from 1 to n, where i=1 means dorm 1, i=n means dorm n
   - Room number = b - prefix[i-1]

Wait, no: prefix[i] is the sum of first i dorms.
So if prefix[i] >= b, then b is in dorm i or later, but we want the first dorm such that prefix[i] >= b.

Actually, b is in dorm k if prefix[k-1] < b <= prefix[k].

So we want to find k such that prefix[k-1] < b <= prefix[k].

We can binary search for the smallest k such that prefix[k] >= b, which gives us k, and then b - prefix[k-1] is the room number.

Yes.

So code:

```python
import bisect

n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Build prefix sums: prefix[i] = sum of first i elements of a
# prefix[0] = 0, prefix[1] = a[0], prefix[2] = a[0]+a[1], etc.
prefix = [0]
for i in range(n):
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

# For each query b_j, find the dormitory
for room in b:
    # Find the smallest index i (0 <= i <= n) such that prefix[i] >= room
    # This will give us the dormitory index (1-indexed)
    idx = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, room)
    # idx is the smallest index where prefix[idx] >= room
    # So the dormitory is idx (1-indexed, since idx=1 means first dorm)
    dorm = idx
    # Room number within dorm: room - prefix[dorm-1]
    room_in_dorm = room - prefix[dorm - 1]
    print(dorm, room_in_dorm)
```

Let me verify with the first example:
- n=3, m=6
- a = [10, 15, 12]
- prefix = [0, 10, 25, 37]
- b = [1, 9, 12, 23, 26, 37]

For room=1:
- bisect_left(prefix, 1) → first index where prefix[i] >= 1 → prefix[1]=10 >= 1 → idx=1
- dorm = 1
- room_in_dorm = 1 - prefix[0] = 1 - 0 = 1 → (1,1) ✓

For room=9:
- prefix[1]=10 >= 9 → idx=1
- dorm=1
- room_in_dorm = 9 - prefix[0] = 9 → (1,9) ✓

For room=12:
- prefix[1]=10 < 12, prefix[2]=25 >= 12 → idx=2
- dorm=2
- room_in_dorm = 12 - prefix[1] = 12 - 10 = 2 → (2,2) ✓

For room=23:
- prefix[1]=10 < 23, prefix[2]=25 >= 23 → idx=2
- dorm=2
- room_in_dorm = 23 - prefix[1] = 23 - 10 = 13 → (2,13) ✓

For room=26:
- prefix[2]=25 < 26, prefix[3]=37 >= 26 → idx=3
- dorm=3
- room_in_dorm = 26 - prefix[2] = 26 - 25 = 1 → (3,1) ✓

For room=37:
- prefix[3]=37 >= 37 → idx=3
- dorm=3
- room_in_dorm = 37 - prefix[2] = 37 - 25 = 12 → (3,12) ✓

Perfect! Matches expected output.

Let me check the second example:
- n=2, m=3
- a = [5, 10000000000]
- prefix = [0, 5, 10000000005]
- b = [5, 6, 9999999999]

For room=5:
- prefix[1]=5 >= 5 → idx=1
- dorm=1
- room_in_dorm = 5 - prefix[0] = 5 → (1,5) ✓

For room=6:
- prefix[1]=5 < 6, prefix[2]=10000000005 >= 6 → idx=2
- dorm=2
- room_in_dorm = 6 - prefix[1] = 6 - 5 = 1 → (2,1) ✓

For room=9999999999:
- prefix[2]=10000000005 >= 9999999999 → idx=2
- dorm=2
- room_in_dorm = 9999999999 - prefix[1] = 9999999999 - 5 = 9999999994 → (2,9999999994) ✓

Perfect! Matches expected output.

```python
import bisect

n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Build prefix sums: prefix[i] = sum of first i elements of a
# prefix[0] = 0, prefix[1] = a[0], prefix[2] = a[0]+a[1], etc.

    # Find the smallest index i (0 <= i <= n) such that prefix[i] >= room
    idx = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, room)
    # idx is the dormitory number (1-indexed)
    dorm = idx
    # Room number within dorm: room - prefix[dorm-1]
    room_in_dorm = room - prefix[dorm - 1]
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` dormitories, each with `a_i` rooms
2. Rooms are numbered sequentially across all dormitories: first dormitory rooms 1 to a₁, then dormitory 2 rooms a₁+1 to a₁+a₂, and so on
3. For each letter with a room number among all rooms, we need to find which dormitory and which room number within that dormitory it should go to

## Key Insights

1. We need to find which dormitory contains a given room number (by doing a prefix sum check)
2. Once we find the dormitory, we can calculate the room number within that dormitory by subtracting the sum of all previous dormitories

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Prefix Sum Array**: Create a prefix sum array where prefix[i] = a₁ + a₂ + ... + aᵢ. This tells us the total number of rooms in the first i dormitories.

2. **For each query**: Given a room number `b_j`, we need to find the smallest index `i` such that prefix[i] >= b_j. This tells us which dormitory the room belongs to.

3. **Room calculation**: Once we know the dormitory index `i`, the room number within that dormitory is `b_j - prefix[i-1]`.

Wait, let me reconsider the prefix sum definition:
- prefix[0] = 0
- prefix[1] = a₁
- prefix[2] = a₁ + a₂
- prefix[i] = sum of first i dormitories

So if a room number `x` falls in dormitory `i`, then:
- prefix[i-1] < x <= prefix[i]
- Room number in dormitory i = x - prefix[i-1]

## Algorithm

1. Read n, m
2. Read array a of dormitory sizes
3. Build prefix sum array: prefix[0] = 0, prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i-1] for i from 1 to n
4. For each query b_j:
   - Find the dormitory index by finding the first position where prefix[i] >= b_j
   - Calculate room number = b_j - prefix[i-1]
   - Output (i, room number)

Since the queries are sorted, we can use binary search to find the dormitory efficiently.

## Implementation

```python
import bisect

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Build prefix sum array
# prefix[i] = sum of first i dormitories (rooms from 1 to prefix[i])
prefix = [0]
for i in range(n):
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

# For each query, find which dormitory it belongs to
# We need to find the smallest index i such that prefix[i] >= b_j
# This is exactly what bisect.bisect_right gives us for the prefix array
for room_num in b:
    # Find the position where room_num would be inserted in prefix array
    # bisect_right returns the insertion point after the last element <= room_num
    # But we want the first prefix[i] >= room_num
    
    # Actually, we can use bisect_left on the prefix array excluding the first element
    # We need the first index i where prefix[i] >= room_num
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, room_num)
    
    # pos is the dormitory index (1-indexed)
    dormitory = pos  # because prefix[0]=0, so pos=1 means first dormitory
    
    # Room number within dormitory = room_num - sum of rooms in previous dormitories
    # Previous dormitory sum is prefix[pos-1]
    room_in_dorm = room_num - prefix[pos-1]
    
    print(dormitory, room_in_dorm)
```

Let me trace through the first example to verify:
- n=3, m=6
- a = [10, 15, 12]
- b = [1, 9, 12, 23, 26, 37]
- prefix = [0, 10, 25, 37]

For room_num = 1:
- bisect_left(prefix, 1) = 1 (since prefix[0]=0 < 1, prefix[1]=10 >= 1)
- dormitory = 1
- room_in_dorm = 1 - prefix[0] = 1 - 0 = 1 ✓

For room_num = 9:
- bisect_left(prefix, 9) = 1 (since prefix[0]=0 < 9, prefix[1]=10 >= 9)
- dormitory = 1
- room_in_dorm = 9 - prefix[0] = 9 ✓

For room_num = 12:
- bisect_left(prefix, 12) = 2 (since prefix[0]=0 < 12, prefix[1]=10 < 12, prefix[2]=25 >= 12)
- dormitory = 2
- room_in_dorm = 12 - prefix[1] = 12 - 10 = 2 ✓

But let me check the expected output: it shows "2 2" for the third letter, which matches.

Actually looking back: the output says:

2 2
2 13
3 1

So for room 12: dorm 2, room 2 - this is correct.

For room 23:
- bisect_left(prefix, 23) = 3 (prefix[0]=0, prefix[1]=10, prefix[2]=25 >= 23)
- dormitory = 3
- room_in_dorm = 23 - prefix[2] = 23 - 25 = -2 ❌

Wait, this is wrong! 23 should be in dorm 3, but prefix[2] = 25, so 23 - 25 = -2, which is impossible.

Let me recalculate the prefix sum:
- dorm 1: 10 rooms → 1-10
- dorm 2: 15 rooms → 11-25
- dorm 3: 12 rooms → 26-37

So:
- room 12: 11-25 → dorm 2, room 12-10 = 2 → correct
- room 23: 11-25 → dorm 2, room 23-10 = 13 → should be room 13 in dorm 2

But in the expected output it says "2 13" for the fourth letter, which is correct.

- prefix[0] = 0
- prefix[1] = 10
- prefix[2] = 10 + 15 = 25
- prefix[3] = 25 + 12 = 37

So for room 23:
- bisect_left(prefix, 23) = 2 because prefix[1]=10 < 23, prefix[2]=25 >= 23
- dormitory = 2
- room_in_dorm = 23 - prefix[1] = 23 - 10 = 13 ✓

Perfect! For room 26:
- prefix[0]=0, prefix[1]=10, prefix[2]=25, prefix[3]=37
- bisect_left(prefix, 26) = 3 (since 25 < 26, 37 >= 26)
- dormitory = 3
- room_in_dorm = 26 - prefix[2] = 26 - 25 = 1 ✓

For room 37:
- bisect_left(prefix, 37) = 3
- dormitory = 3
- room_in_dorm = 37 - prefix[2] = 37 - 25 = 12 ✓

Perfect! All match.

```python
import bisect

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Build prefix sum array
prefix = [0]
for i in range(n):
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

# For each query, find which dormitory it belongs to
for room_num in b:
    # Find the smallest index i such that prefix[i] >= room_num
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, room_num)
    # pos is 1-indexed dormitory number
    dormitory = pos
    # Room number within the dormitory
    room_in_dorm = room_num - prefix[pos - 1]
    print(dormitory, room_in_dorm)
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There are $n$ dormitories in Berland State University, they are numbered with integers from $1$ to $n$. Each dormitory consists of rooms, there are $a_i$ rooms in $i$-th dormitory. The rooms in $i$-th dormitory are numbered from $1$ to $a_i$.

For example, in case $n=2$, $a_1=3$ and $a_2=5$ an envelope can have any integer from $1$ to $8$ written on it. If the number $7$ is written on an envelope, it means that the letter should be delivered to the room number $4$ of the second dormitory.

The third line contains a sequence $b_1, b_2, \dots, b_m$ $(1 \le b_j \le a_1 + a_2 + \dots + a_n)$, where $b_j$ equals to the room number (among all rooms of all dormitories) for the $j$-th letter. I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` dormitories, each with `a_i` rooms
2. Rooms are numbered consecutively across all dormitories: first all rooms in dorm 1, then dorm 2, etc.
3. For each letter with a room number (among all rooms), we need to find which dormitory and which room within that dormitory it belongs to
4. The room numbers are given in increasing order

## Key Insight

To find which dormitory a room belongs to, we need to:
1. Precompute the cumulative sum of rooms - this tells us the end boundary of each dormitory
2. For each query room number `b_j`, find the first dormitory `f` such that the cumulative sum up to dorm `f-1` < `b_j` ≤ cumulative sum up to dorm `f`
3. The room number within that dormitory is `b_j - cumulative_sum[f-1]`

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input values
2. Calculate prefix sums (cumulative room counts) for each dormitory
3. For each room number in the queries, use binary search or linear search to find which dormitory it belongs to
4. Since the queries are sorted, we could potentially use binary search, but given constraints, even linear might work due to the sorted nature

However, since `m` can be up to 2×10^5 and we have to process each query, and the dormitory structure might be sparse, we can use binary search to find the correct dormitory for each query.

But actually, since queries are sorted, we can process them sequentially and use the cumulative sums to find the right dormitory.

Wait, let me reconsider: we have `m` queries, each requiring finding which dormitory. We can precompute the cumulative sums, then for each query, we can binary search for the first position where the cumulative sum >= query value.

Alternatively, since queries are sorted, we might be able to do a single pass through the dormitories and queries, but given that queries can jump across dorms, binary search is safer.

Let me implement with binary search for efficiency.

## Algorithm

1. Read `n`, `m`
2. Read array `a` of room counts per dormitory
3. Compute prefix sums: `prefix[i] = a[0] + a[1] + ... + a[i-1]` (prefix[0] = 0, prefix[1] = a[0], etc.)
4. For each query value `b_j`, find the smallest index `f` (1-indexed) such that `prefix[f] >= b_j`
5. The dormitory is `f`, and the room number is `b_j - prefix[f-1]`

## Implementation

```python
import bisect

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
queries = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Precompute prefix sums
# prefix[i] represents the total number of rooms in dorms 1 to i
prefix = [0]
for i in range(n):
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

# For each query, find which dormitory it belongs to
# We need to find the first index f such that prefix[f] >= query_value
# This f is the dormitory number (1-indexed)
results = []
for b in queries:
    # Find the leftmost position where prefix[i] >= b
    # Since prefix is sorted, we can use bisect
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, b)
    # pos is the 0-indexed position, so dormitory number is pos + 1
    dormitory = pos + 1
    # Room number within the dormitory is b - prefix[pos-1] 
    # But when pos == 0, prefix[pos-1] is prefix[-1], which is 0
    # Actually, prefix[pos] is the cumulative sum up to dorm pos
    # So the room number is b - prefix[pos-1] where prefix[0] = 0
    # When pos = 0, we use prefix[-1] which is 0
    # When pos > 0, we use prefix[pos-1]
    prev_cumulative = prefix[pos - 1] if pos > 0 else 0
    room_in_dorm = b - prev_cumulative
    results.append((dormitory, room_in_dorm))

# Output results
for dorm, room in results:
    print(dorm, room)
```

import bisect

```python
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

results = []
for b in queries:
    # Find the leftmost index in prefix where prefix[i] >= b
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, b)
    # pos is the 0-indexed index in prefix array
    # This means the letter is in dorm number pos (1-indexed)
    dormitory = pos
    # The room number within the dormitory is b - (total rooms before this dorm)
    # Total rooms before dorm pos is prefix[pos-1]
    # For pos=1, prefix[0] = 0
    prev_cumulative = prefix[pos - 1] if pos > 0 else 0
    room_in_dorm = b - prev_cumulative
    results.append((dormitory, room_in_dorm))

for dorm, room in results:
```

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
queries = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Precompute prefix sums: prefix[i] is the total rooms from dorm 1 to i
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There are $n$ dormitories in Berland State University, they are numbered with integers from $1$ to $n$. Each dormitory consists of rooms, there are $a_i$ rooms in $i$-th dormitory. The rooms in $i$-th dormitory are numbered from $1$ to $a_i$.

A postman delivers letters. Sometimes there is no specific dormitory and room number in it on an envelope. Instead of it only a room number among all rooms of all $n$ dormitories is written on an envelope. In this case, assume that all the rooms are numbered from $1$ to $a_1 + a_2 + \dots + a_n$ and the rooms of the first dormitory go first, the rooms of the second dormitory go after them and so on.

For example, in case $n=2$, $a_1=3$ and $a_2=5$ an envelope can have any integer from $1$ to $8$ written on it. If the number $7$ is written on an envelope, it means that the letter should be delivered to the room number $4$ of the second dormitory.

For each of $m$ letters by the room number among all $n$ dormitories, determine the particular dormitory and the room number in a dormitory where this letter should be delivered.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers $n$ and $m$ $(1 \le n, m \le 2 \cdot 10^{5})$ — the number of dormitories and the number of letters.

The second line contains a sequence $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ $(1 \le a_i \le 10^{10})$, where $a_i$ equals to the number of rooms in the $i$-th dormitory. The third line contains a sequence $b_1, b_2, \dots, b_m$ $(1 \le b_j \le a_1 + a_2 + \dots + a_n)$, where $b_j$ equals to the room number (among all rooms of all dormitories) for the $j$-th letter. All $b_j$ are given in increasing order.


-----Output-----

Print $m$ lines. For each letter print two integers $f$ and $k$ — the dormitory number $f$ $(1 \le f \le n)$ and the room number $k$ in this dormitory $(1 \le k \le a_f)$ to deliver the letter.


-----Examples-----
Input
3 6
10 15 12
1 9 12 23 26 37

Output
1 1
1 9
2 2
2 13
3 1
3 12

Input
2 3
5 10000000000
5 6 9999999999

Output
1 5
2 1
2 9999999994



-----Note-----

In the first example letters should be delivered in the following order:  the first letter in room $1$ of the first dormitory  the second letter in room $9$ of the first dormitory  the third letter in room $2$ of the second dormitory  the fourth letter in room $13$ of the second dormitory  the fifth letter in room $1$ of the third dormitory  the sixth letter in room $12$ of the third dormitory

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` dormitories, each with `a_i` rooms
2. Rooms are numbered sequentially across all dormitories: first all rooms of dormitory 1, then dormitory 2, etc.
3. For each letter with a room number `b_j`, we need to find which dormitory and which room within that dormitory it belongs to
4. The dormitories are numbered 1 to n, and rooms in dormitory i are numbered 1 to a_i

## Solution Approach

1. First, I need to compute the prefix sums of the dormitory sizes to know where each dormitory starts and ends in the global room numbering
2. For each query room number `b_j`, I need to find which dormitory it falls into by checking which range it belongs to
3. Once I find the dormitory, I can calculate the room number within that dormitory by subtracting the sum of all previous dormitories

## Step-by-step Algorithm

1. Read n, m and the array a of dormitory sizes
2. Compute prefix sums: `prefix[i]` = sum of a[0] to a[i-1] (which represents the end of dormitory i)
3. For each query b_j:
   - Find the smallest index i such that prefix[i] >= b_j
   - This means b_j falls in dormitory i+1 (since dormitory indexing is 1-based)
   - The room number within dormitory i+1 is b_j - prefix[i-1] (where prefix[i-1] is the sum before dormitory i+1)

Let me trace through the first example:
- n=3, m=6
- a = [10, 15, 12]
- prefix = [0, 10, 25, 37] (prefix[0]=0, prefix[1]=10, prefix[2]=25, prefix[3]=37)

For b_j = 1:
- Find smallest i where prefix[i] >= 1 → i=1 (prefix[1]=10 >= 1)
- Dormitory = i = 1 (1-indexed)
- Room = 1 - prefix[0] = 1 - 0 = 1 → (1,1)

For b_j = 9:

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Compute prefix sums of dormitory sizes
# prefix[i] = sum of a[0] to a[i-1]
prefix = [0]
for i in range(n):
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

# For each query, find which dormitory it belongs to
for room in b:
    # Find the smallest index i such that prefix[i] >= room
    # This means room belongs to dormitory i (1-indexed)
    # prefix[i] is the cumulative sum up to and including dormitory i
    left, right = 0, n
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if prefix[mid] < room:
            left = mid + 1
        else:
            right = mid
    
    # Now left is the index such that prefix[left] >= room
    # The dormitory number is left (1-indexed)
    dormitory = left
    # The room number within dormitory is room - prefix[dormitory-1]
    room_in_dorm = room - prefix[dormitory - 1]
    
    print(dormitory, room_in_dorm)
```

Wait, I can optimize this further. Since the queries are sorted, I could also use binary search for each one, but the problem size is up to 2*10^5, so binary search for each query (O(m log n)) is acceptable.

But actually, I can make it simpler: I can iterate through the prefix array to find the first prefix that is >= room.

However, binary search is more efficient for large n.

Let me stick with binary search since it's O(log n) per query.

Actually, I can also use a simpler approach: since prefix is sorted, I can use bisect_left.

But since we're not allowed to import modules, I'll implement binary search manually.

The code above should work correctly.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Compute prefix sums of dormitory sizes
prefix = [0]
for i in range(n):
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

# For each query, find which dormitory it belongs to
for room in b:
    # Binary search for the first index where prefix[i] >= room
    left, right = 0, n
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if prefix[mid] >= room:
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid + 1
    
    # left is now the index such that prefix[left] >= room
    # dormitory number is left (1-indexed)
    dormitory = left
    # room number within dormitory = room - prefix[dormitory-1]
    room_in_dorm = room - prefix[dormitory - 1]
    
    print(dormitory, room_in_dorm)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There are $n$ dormitories in Berland State University, they are numbered with integers from $1$ to $n$. Each dormitory consists of rooms, there are $a_i$ rooms in $i$-th dormitory. The rooms in $i$-th dormitory are numbered from $1$ to $a_i$.

A postman delivers letters. Sometimes there is no specific dormitory and room number in it on an envelope. Instead of it only a room number among all rooms of all $n$ dormitories is written on an envelope. In this case, assume that all the rooms are numbered from $1$ to $a_1 + a_2 + \dots + a_n$ and the rooms of the first dormitory go first, the rooms of the second dormitory go after them and so on.

For example, in case $n=2$, $a_1=3$ and $a_2=5$ an envelope can have any integer from $1$ to $8$ written on it. If the number $7$ is written on an envelope, it means that the letter should be delivered to the room number $4$ of the second dormitory.

For each of $m$ letters by the room number among all $n$ dormitories, determine the particular dormitory and the room number in a dormitory where this letter should be delivered.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers $n$ and $m$ $(1 \le n, m \le 2 \cdot 10^{5})$ — the number of dormitories and the number of letters.

The second line contains a sequence $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ $(1 \le a_i \le 10^{10})$, where $a_i$ equals to the number of rooms in the $i$-th dormitory. The third line contains a sequence $b_1, b_2, \dots, b_m$ $(1 \le b_j \le a_1 + a_2 + \dots + a_n)$, where $b_j$ equals to the room number (among all rooms of all dormitories) for the $j$-th letter. All $b_j$ are given in increasing order.


-----Output-----

Print $m$ lines. For each letter print two integers $f$ and $k$ — the dormitory number $f$ $(1 \le f \le n)$ and the room number $k$ in this dormitory $(1 \le k \le a_f)$ to deliver the letter.


-----Examples-----
Input
3 6
10 15 12
1 9 12 23 26 37

Output
1 1
1 9
2 2
2 13
3 1
3 12

Input
2 3
5 10000000000
5 6 9999999999

Output
1 5
2 1
2 9999999994



-----Note-----

In the first example letters should be delivered in the following order:  the first letter in room $1$ of the first dormitory  the second letter in room $9$ of the first dormitory  the third letter in room $2$ of the second dormitory  the fourth letter in room $13$ of the second dormitory  the fifth letter in room $1$ of the third dormitory  the sixth letter in room $12$ of the third dormitory

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` dormitories, each with `a_i` rooms
2. Rooms are numbered sequentially across all dormitories: first all rooms of dormitory 1, then dormitory 2, etc.
3. For each letter with a room number (among all rooms), we need to find which dormitory and which room within that dormitory it belongs to
4. The input gives us a sorted list of room numbers, and we need to map each to (dormitory, room number)

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Understand the mapping
- Total rooms = sum of all a_i
- If a room number is in the range [1, a_1], it's in dormitory 1
- If a room number is in the range [a_1+1, a_1+a_2], it's in dormitory 2
- And so on...

### Step 2: Algorithm approach
1. Precompute prefix sums of the dormitory sizes to know the boundaries
2. For each query room number, find which dormitory it falls into by binary search or linear scan
3. Once we know the dormitory, calculate the room number within that dormitory

### Step 3: Implementation details
- Create prefix sum array where prefix[i] = a_1 + a_2 + ... + a_i
- For a given room number, find the smallest index i such that prefix[i] >= room number
- This i will be the dormitory number
- The room number within that dormitory = room_number - prefix[i-1]

### Step 4: Edge case handling
- Since dormitories are 1-indexed, we need to be careful with indexing
- The prefix sum array will help us find the boundary

Let me trace through the first example:
- n=3, m=6, a=[10,15,12]
- prefix = [10, 25, 37] (cumulative sums)
- Room 1: 1 <= 10, so dorm 1, room 1
- Room 9: 1 <= 9 <= 10, so dorm 1, room 9  
- Room 12: 11 <= 12 <= 25, so dorm 2, room 12-10 = 2
- Wait, room 12 should be room 2 in dorm 2? Let me check the expected output...

Looking at expected output: 12 should go to room 2 of dorm 2? But expected output says "2 2" for room 12, which is correct.

But the example says room 12 goes to dorm 2, room 2. That means room 11 would be room 1, room 12 would be room 2. Yes.

Wait, the output shows: 12 -> 2 2, which means room 2 of dorm 2. That's correct since dorm 1 has 10 rooms (1-10), so room 11-25 is dorm 2.

But the input shows 12, 23, 26, 37. Let me check 23:
- 23 > 10, 23 <= 25, so dorm 2, room 23-10 = 13 → should be "2 13" ✓
- 26: 26 > 25, 26 <= 37, so dorm 3, room 26-25 = 1 → "3 1" ✓
- 37: 37 <= 37, so dorm 3, room 37-25 = 12 → "3 12" ✓

Perfect!

### Step 5: Implementation

Since m can be up to 2*10^5 and we have to process each query, we can either:
- Use binary search for each query (O(m log n))
- Or precompute the prefix sums and use linear scan (O(m n) which might be too slow)

Given that n and m are both up to 2*10^5, O(m log n) is acceptable.

Actually, we can do even better - since the queries are sorted, we could use binary search on the prefix array.

But since n is small (at most 2*10^5), and we have only one prefix array, we can just precompute the prefix sums and then for each query, do a linear scan through dormitories or use binary search.

I'll use binary search for efficiency.

Wait, actually since the room numbers are already sorted, but we don't need to sort them again. We just need to find for each query which dormitory it belongs to.

The simplest approach is to precompute the prefix sums, then for each query, find the dormitory using binary search (since the prefix array is sorted).

```python
import bisect

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Precompute prefix sums: prefix[i] = sum of a[0] to a[i-1]
# prefix[0] = 0, prefix[1] = a[0], prefix[2] = a[0] + a[1], etc.
prefix = [0]
for i in range(n):
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

# For each query room number, find which dormitory it belongs to
# We need to find the smallest index i such that prefix[i] >= room_number
# This will give us the dormitory (index i, since dormitories are 1-indexed)
for room in b:
    # Find the first prefix sum >= room
    idx = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, room)
    # idx will be the dormitory index (1-indexed)
    dorm = idx
    # Room number within dormitory = room - prefix[idx-1]
    room_in_dorm = room - prefix[dorm - 1]
    print(dorm, room_in_dorm)
```

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1:
- prefix = [0, 10, 25, 37]
- room=1: bisect_left(prefix, 1) = 1, dorm=1, room_in_dorm=1-0=1 → "1 1" ✓
- room=9: bisect_left(prefix, 9) = 1, dorm=1, room_in_dorm=9-0=9 → "1 9" ✓
- room=12: bisect_left(prefix, 12) = 2 (since 12 >= 10, and 12 < 25), dorm=2, room_in_dorm=12-10=2 → "2 2" ✓
- room=23: bisect_left(prefix, 23) = 2 (23 >= 10, 23 < 25? 23 < 25 is true, so should be 2. But 23 >= 10 is true, and prefix[2]=25 >= 23, so when we search for first >=23, it should be at index 2. So dorm=2, room_in_dorm=23-10=13 → "2 13" ✓
- room=26: bisect_left(prefix, 26) = 3 (since 26 >= 25), dorm=3, room_in_dorm=26-25=1 → "3 1" ✓
- room=37: bisect_left(prefix, 37) = 3 (37 >= 37), dorm=3, room_in_dorm=37-25=12 → "3 12" ✓

Perfect! The example matches.

Example 2:
- n=2, m=3, a=[5, 10000000000]
- prefix = [0, 5, 10000000005]
- room=5: bisect_left(prefix, 5) = 1, dorm=1, room_in_dorm=5-0=5 → "1 5" ✓
- room=6: bisect_left(prefix, 6) = 2 (6 >= 5), dorm=2, room_in_dorm=6-5=1 → "2 1" ✓
- room=9999999999: bisect_left(prefix, 9999999999) = 2 (since 9999999999 >= 5 and 9999999999 < 10000000005), so dorm=2, room_in_dorm=9999999999-5=9999999994 → "2 9999999994" ✓

Perfect match!

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` dormitories with `a_i` rooms in the i-th dormitory
2. Rooms are numbered sequentially across all dormitories: first all rooms of dorm 1, then dorm 2, etc.
3. For each letter with a room number among all dormitories, we need to find which dormitory and which room within that dormitory it belongs to
4. The input gives us the total room numbers (1 to sum of all a_i) and we need to map each to a specific dormitory and room number

## Approach

1. First, I need to compute the prefix sums of the dormitory room counts. This will give me the cumulative number of rooms up to each dormitory.
2. For each query room number, I need to find which dormitory it falls into by checking where it lies in the prefix sum array.
3. Once I know which dormitory (f), I can calculate the room number within that dormitory by subtracting the rooms in all previous dormitories.

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Read input
- Read n, m
- Read array a of room counts for each dormitory
- Read array b of room numbers for each letter

### Step 2: Compute prefix sums
- Create a prefix sum array where prefix[i] = a[0] + a[1] + ... + a[i-1]
- This tells us how many rooms are in dormitories 1 through i

### Step 3: For each query room number
- Find the dormitory by binary search or linear search (since m and n are up to 2×10^5, and queries are sorted, we can use binary search for efficiency)
- Once we know the dormitory index, calculate the room number within that dormitory

### Step 4: Output the results

- n=3, m=6
- a = [10, 15, 12]
- prefix sums: [0, 10, 25, 37]
- Query: 1 → falls in dorm 1 (1 ≤ 10), room = 1 - 0 = 1 → (1,1)
- Query: 9 → falls in dorm 1 (1 ≤ 9 ≤ 10), room = 9 - 0 = 9 → (1,9)
- Query: 12 → falls in dorm 2? 10 < 12 ≤ 25, so dorm 2, room = 12 - 10 = 2 → (2,2)
- Wait, this doesn't match the expected output. Expected is (2,13) for 23.

- Dorm 1: rooms 1-10
- Dorm 2: rooms 11-25
- Dorm 3: rooms 26-37

For query 26: 26 is in dorm 3 (26 ≤ 37), room = 26 - 25 = 1 → (3,1) ✓
For query 37: 37 is in dorm 3, room = 37 - 25 = 12 → (3,12) ✓

The algorithm is correct.

Since the queries are given in increasing order, I can use binary search to find the dormitory efficiently, or even just iterate through the prefix sums.

But since n and m are up to 2×10^5, and we have m queries, a linear scan for each query would be O(m×n) which might be too slow.

Better approach: I can precompute the prefix sums, then for each query, do a binary search to find which dormitory it belongs to.

Actually, with m, n ≤ 2×10^5, O(m×n) = 4×10^10 operations which is way too slow.

So I should use binary search on the prefix sum array.

Alternative: Since queries are sorted, I can iterate through the prefix sums once and use binary search for each query.

But there's an even simpler approach: precompute the prefix sums, then for each query, just iterate through the prefix sums until I find the first prefix that is ≥ query room number.

Since the prefix sum array is increasing, this is O(n) per query, which is O(m×n) worst case.

Actually, O(m×n) with m,n = 2×10^5 would be 4×10^10 operations, which is too slow in Python.

I need a more efficient solution. I can use binary search on the prefix sum array for each query.

The prefix sum array has size n+1, so binary search per query is O(m × log n), which is acceptable.

## Implementation

```python
import bisect

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Compute prefix sums
# prefix[i] = sum of rooms from dorm 1 to dorm i
prefix = [0]
for i in range(n):
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

# For each query room number, find which dormitory it belongs to
for room in b:
    # Find the first index where prefix[i] >= room
    # This gives us the dormitory index (1-indexed)
    # Actually, we want the smallest i such that prefix[i] >= room
    # Then dormitory number is i (1-indexed)
    idx = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, room)
    # Dormitory number is idx (1-indexed because prefix[0]=0, prefix[1]=a[0], etc.)
    dormitory = idx
    # Room number in that dormitory = room - prefix[dormitory-1]
    room_in_dorm = room - prefix[dormitory - 1]
    
    print(dormitory, room_in_dorm)
```

- a = [10,15,12], so prefix = [0,10,25,37]

  - dormitory = 1, room_in_dorm = 1 - prefix[0] = 1 - 0 = 1 ✓
- room = 9: bisect_left(prefix,9) returns 1 (since 0<9, 10>=9)
  - dormitory = 1, room_in_dorm = 9 - prefix[0] = 9 ✓

- prefix = [0,10,25,37]
- bisect_left(prefix, 23) returns 2 because prefix[1]=10 < 23, prefix[2]=25 >= 23
- dormitory = 2, room_in_dorm = 23 - prefix[1] = 23 - 10 = 13 ✓

Perfect! It works.

For room = 26:
- bisect_left(prefix, 26) returns 3 because prefix[2]=25 < 26, prefix[3]=37 >= 26
- dormitory = 3, room_in_dorm = 26 - prefix[2] = 26 - 25 = 1 ✓

For room = 37:
- bisect_left(prefix, 37) returns 3 (prefix[3]=37 >= 37)
- dormitory = 3, room_in_dorm = 37 - prefix[2] = 37 - 25 = 12 ✓

Perfect match with expected output.

Let me check the second example:
- n=2, m=3
- a = [5, 10000000000]
- prefix = [0, 5, 10000000005]
- b = [5, 6, 9999999999]

For room=5: bisect_left(prefix,5) returns 1, dormitory=1, room_in_dorm=5-0=5 ✓
For room=6: bisect_left(prefix,6) returns 2 (since 5<6, 10000000005>=6), dormitory=2, room_in_dorm=6-5=1 ✓
For room=9999999999: bisect_left(prefix,9999999999) returns 2, dormitory=2, room_in_dorm=9999999999-5=9999999994 ✓

```python
import bisect

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Compute prefix sums
prefix = [0]
for i in range(n):
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

# For each query room number, find which dormitory it belongs to
for room in b:
    # Find the smallest index where prefix[index] >= room
    dormitory_idx = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, room)
    # Dormitory number is dormitory_idx (1-indexed)
    # Room number within dormitory = room - sum of all previous dormitories
    room_in_dorm = room - prefix[dormitory_idx - 1]
    print(dormitory_idx, room_in_dorm)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There are $n$ dormitories in Berland State University, they are numbered with integers from $1$ to $n$. Each dormitory consists of rooms, there are $a_i$ rooms in $i$-th dormitory. The rooms in $i$-th dormitory are numbered from $1$ to $a_i$.

A postman delivers letters. Sometimes there is no specific dormitory and room number in it on an envelope. Instead of it only a room number among all rooms of all $n$ dormitories is written on an envelope. In this case, assume that all the rooms are numbered from $1$ to $a_1 + a_2 + \dots + a_n$ and the rooms of the first dormitory go first, the rooms of the second dormitory go after them and so on.

For example, in case $n=2$, $a_1=3$ and $a_2=5$ an envelope can have any integer from $1$ to $8$ written on it. If the number $7$ is written on an envelope, it means that the letter should be delivered to the room number $4$ of the second dormitory.

For each of $m$ letters by the room number among all $n$ dormitories, determine the particular dormitory and the room number in a dormitory where this letter should be delivered.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers $n$ and $m$ $(1 \le n, m \le 2 \cdot 10^{5})$ — the number of dormitories and the number of letters.

The second line contains a sequence $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ $(1 \le a_i \le 10^{10})$, where $a_i$ equals to the number of rooms in the $i$-th dormitory. The third line contains a sequence $b_1, b_2, \dots, b_m$ $(1 \le b_j \le a_1 + a_2 + \dots + a_n)$, where $b_j$ equals to the room number (among all rooms of all dormitories) for the $j$-th letter. All $b_j$ are given in increasing order.


-----Output-----

Print $m$ lines. For each letter print two integers $f$ and $k$ — the dormitory number $f$ $(1 \le f \le n)$ and the room number $k$ in this dormitory $(1 \le k \le a_f)$ to deliver the letter.


-----Examples-----
Input
3 6
10 15 12
1 9 12 23 26 37

Output
1 1
1 9
2 2
2 13
3 1
3 12

Input
2 3
5 10000000000
5 6 9999999999

Output
1 5
2 1
2 9999999994



-----Note-----

In the first example letters should be delivered in the following order:  the first letter in room $1$ of the first dormitory  the second letter in room $9$ of the first dormitory  the third letter in room $2$ of the second dormitory  the fourth letter in room $13$ of the second dormitory  the fifth letter in room $1$ of the third dormitory  the sixth letter in room $12$ of the third dormitory

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` dormitories, each with `a_i` rooms
2. Rooms are numbered sequentially across all dormitories: first all rooms of dorm 1, then dorm 2, etc.
3. For each letter with a room number (among all rooms), we need to find which dormitory and which room within that dormitory it belongs to
4. The room numbers are given in increasing order

## Approach

The key insight is that we need to find which dormitory contains a given global room number. 

For example, if dormitory 1 has 10 rooms, dormitory 2 has 15 rooms, and dormitory 3 has 12 rooms:
- Global room 1-10 → dorm 1, rooms 1-10
- Global room 11-25 → dorm 2, rooms 1-15
- Global room 26-37 → dorm 3, rooms 1-12

So for a given global room number `b_j`, we need to find the smallest dormitory index `f` such that the sum of rooms from dormitory 1 to dormitory `f` is greater than or equal to `b_j`.

This can be solved using binary search or by pre-computing prefix sums.

Since we have up to 200,000 letters and dormitories, and room counts up to 10^10, we can:
1. Pre-compute prefix sums of room counts to quickly determine range boundaries
2. For each query, find which dormitory range contains the given room number

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input: n, m, array a of room counts, array b of queries
2. Pre-compute prefix sums (cumulative room counts) for all dormitories
3. For each query room number, find which dormitory it belongs to by checking prefix sums
4. Calculate the room number within that dormitory

## Implementation Strategy

Instead of doing binary search for each query (which would be O(m log n)), we can use the fact that the queries are sorted. However, even simpler: for each query, we can iterate through dormitories (or use binary search) to find where it falls.

Since n and m can be up to 2×10^5, and we have m queries, a naive O(n×m) solution would be too slow.

Better approach: Pre-compute prefix sums, then for each query, use binary search to find the dormitory.

But actually, since the queries are sorted and we only have n dormitories, we can do a linear scan for each query, but that's O(m×n) which is 4×10^10 in worst case - too slow.

Alternative: Pre-compute prefix sums, then for each query, use binary search on the prefix array to find the dormitory.

Let me implement with binary search for efficiency.

Wait, actually we can just precompute the prefix sums and then for each query, iterate through the prefix sums until we find the right dormitory. Since n is only 2×10^5 and m is also 2×10^5, in worst case O(m×n) = 4×10^10 operations, which is too slow in Python.

Better: Precompute prefix array, then for each query, use binary search to find the dormitory. This will be O(m log n).

Let me go with that.

## Algorithm:

1. Precompute prefix sum array `prefix` where `prefix[i]` = sum of rooms from dorm 1 to i
2. For each query `b_j`:
   - Use binary search to find the rightmost dormitory where `prefix[f-1] < b_j <= prefix[f]` 
   - Actually, we need to find the smallest f such that `prefix[f] >= b_j` (where prefix[f] is sum up to f-th dorm)
   - Then the room in that dorm is `b_j - prefix[f-1]`

Note: prefix array should be 1-indexed for dormitory references.

Actually, let me define prefix[0] = 0, prefix[1] = a[0], prefix[2] = a[0]+a[1], etc.

Then for room b_j, we need the smallest index f (1-indexed) such that prefix[f] >= b_j.

Then dorm number is f, and room number is b_j - prefix[f-1].

## Step-by-step with example:

n=3, m=6, a=[10,15,12]
prefix = [0, 10, 25, 37]

Query b_j = 1: find smallest f such that prefix[f] >= 1 → f=1 (prefix[1]=10>=1)
dorm = 1, room = 1 - prefix[0] = 1 - 0 = 1 → (1,1) ✓

Query b_j = 9: f=1 (prefix[1]=10>=9), room = 9 - 0 = 9 → (1,9) ✓

Query b_j = 12: f=2 (prefix[2]=25>=12, prefix[1]=10<12), room = 12 - 10 = 2 → (2,2) ✓

Query b_j = 23: f=2 (prefix[2]=25>=23), room = 23 - 10 = 13 → (2,13) ✓

Query b_j = 26: f=3 (prefix[3]=37>=26), room = 26 - 25 = 1 → (3,1) ✓

Query b_j = 37: f=3, room = 37 - 25 = 12 → (3,12) ✓

Perfect.

So I'll implement binary search for each query.

Alternatively, since the queries are sorted, I could do a two-pointer approach, but binary search per query is O(m log n) which should be acceptable.

```python
import bisect

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Precompute prefix sums
# prefix[i] = sum of rooms from dorm 0 to i-1 (0-indexed)
# prefix[0] = 0
prefix = [0]
for i in range(n):
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

# For each query, find the dormitory using binary search
# We want the smallest index f (1-indexed) such that prefix[f] >= b_j
# This means f is in range [1, n]
for room in b:
    # Find the leftmost position where prefix[i] >= room
    # Since prefix is sorted, we can use bisect_left
    # bisect_left returns the first index where prefix[i] >= room
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, room)
    # pos is 0-indexed, so dorm number is pos+1
    dorm = pos  # 0-indexed position in prefix array
    # But dorm number should be 1-indexed
    # When pos = 0, we have prefix[0] = 0, and prefix[1] = a[0]
    # So if room <= prefix[1], then pos = 1, dorm = 1
    # Actually: if prefix[pos] >= room, then the dorm is pos (0-indexed) but we want 1-indexed dorm number
    
    # The dorm is (pos) in 0-indexed, so dorm number is pos + 1?
    # Wait: if room=1, prefix[0]=0 < 1, prefix[1]=10 >= 1, so pos=1
    # This means room is in dorm 1 (which is a[0]), so dorm number should be 1
    # Since we have n dormitories, pos ranges from 1 to n (in the prefix array)
    
    # Actually, the prefix array has length n+1, indices 0...n
    # prefix[i] represents sum of first i dorms (0-indexed)
    # So if we find pos such that prefix[pos] >= room, then:
    # - The room belongs to dorm number (pos) if we consider 0-indexed dorms
    # But we want 1-indexed dorm number
    # If pos = 1, then it's dorm 1 (first dorm)
    # So dorm number = pos (1-indexed)
    
    # Wait no: when pos = 1, it means the first dorm (index 0) has prefix[1] >= room
    # So the room is in the first dorm (dorm 1)
    # So dorm number is pos (since prefix[1] corresponds to dorm 1)
    
    # Actually, prefix[i] = sum of first i dorms (0-indexed dorms: 0,1,...,i-1)
    # So when we find the smallest i such that prefix[i] >= room, then the room is in dorm i-1 (0-indexed)
    # So dorm number is i (1-indexed)
    
    # Example: room=1, find smallest i such that prefix[i] >= 1
    # prefix[0]=0 < 1
    # prefix[1]=10 >= 1, so i=1
    # dorm number = 1 ✓
    
    # Another: room=11, prefix[0]=0, prefix[1]=10<11, prefix[2]=25>=11, so i=2
    # dorm number = 2 ✓ (dorm 2)
    
    # So dorm number is pos (1-indexed)
    
    dorm_number = pos  # 1-indexed dorm number
    room_in_dorm = room - prefix[pos - 1]  # prefix[pos-1] is sum of first pos-1 dorms
    
    print(dorm_number, room_in_dorm)
```

Wait, there's a bug: when pos=0, we have prefix[0] >= room, but prefix[0]=0, so room would be 0, but room >= 1. So pos will never be 0. The smallest possible pos is 1 since room >=1 and prefix[0]=0 < room.

So in all cases, pos >= 1.

Therefore, `dorm_number = pos` and `room_in_dorm = room - prefix[pos - 1]`

- bisect_left(prefix, 5): prefix[0]=0<5, prefix[1]=5>=5 → pos=1

- room_in_dorm = 5 - prefix[0] = 5 - 0 = 5 → (1,5) ✓

```python
import bisect

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Precompute prefix sums
prefix = [0]
for i in range(n):
    prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])

    pos = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, room)

    dorm_number = pos
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` dormitories, each with `a_i` rooms
2. Rooms are numbered sequentially across all dormitories: rooms 1 to a₁ go to dorm 1, rooms a₁+1 to a₁+a₂ go to dorm 2, etc.

## Approach

2. For each query (global room number), I need to find which dormitory it falls into by binary search or linear search.
3. Once I find the dormitory, the room number within that dormitory is: global_room_number - (sum of rooms in all previous dormitories)

3. **For each query**: Find the dormitory using binary search or linear search (since queries are sorted, we can use binary search for efficiency)

```python
import bisect

n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Precompute prefix sums: prefix[i] = sum of first i dormitories
prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    prefix[i] = prefix[i - 1] + a[i - 1]

# For each query in b, find the leftmost index i such that prefix[i] >= query
# This i will be the dormitory index (1-indexed)
for query in b:
    # Find the first index i in range [0, n] such that prefix[i] >= query
    i = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, query)
    # Dormitory number is i + 1? No
    # i is the index in prefix array
    # When i=0: prefix[0] >= query, so query <= 0, but query >= 1, so i >= 1
    # When i=1: prefix[1] >= query, and prefix[0] < query → dormitory 1
    # When i=2: prefix[2] >= query, and prefix[1] < query → dormitory 2
    # So dormitory number is i
    # Actually, dormitory number = i (since dormitory 1 corresponds to i=1)
    # But when i=1, dormitory should be 1
    # So dormitory number = i
    
    # But wait: prefix[1] = first dormitory sum, so if query is in [1, prefix[1]], dormitory 1
    # So yes, if i=1, dormitory 1
    # If i=2, dormitory 2
    
    # So dormitory number is i
    dormitory = i  # i ranges from 1 to n
    
    # Room number is query - prefix[i-1]
    room = query - prefix[i - 1]
    
    print(dormitory, room)
```

Wait, let me verify with the first example:
- a = [10,15,12], so prefix = [0,10,25,37]
- query = 1: bisect_left(prefix, 1) → first index >= 1
  - prefix[0]=0 < 1, prefix[1]=10 >= 1 → i=1
  - dormitory = 1, room = 1 - prefix[0] = 1 - 0 = 1 ✓
- query = 9: i = bisect_left(prefix, 9) → prefix[1]=10 >= 9 → i=1, room = 9 - prefix[0] = 9 ✓
- query = 12: prefix[1]=10 < 12, prefix[2]=25 >= 12 → i=2, room = 12 - prefix[1] = 12 - 10 = 2 ✓
- query = 23: prefix[1]=10 < 23, prefix[2]=25 >= 23 → i=2, room = 23 - 10 = 13 ✓
- query = 26: prefix[2]=25 < 26, prefix[3]=37 >= 26 → i=3, room = 26 - prefix[2] = 26 - 25 = 1 ✓
- query = 37: i = 3, room = 37 - 25 = 12 ✓

Perfect!

Now the second example:
- n=2, m=3, a=[5,10000000000], b=[5,6,9999999999]
- prefix = [0,5,10000000005]
- query=5: bisect_left(prefix,5) → prefix[1]=5 >= 5 → i=1, dormitory=1, room=5-0=5 ✓
- query=6: prefix[1]=5 < 6, prefix[2]=10000000005 >= 6 → i=2, dormitory=2, room=6-5=1 ✓
- query=9999999999: i such that prefix[i] >= 9999999999
  - prefix[1]=5 < 9999999999, prefix[2]=10000000005 >= 9999999999 → i=2
  - dormitory=2, room=9999999999 - 5 = 9999999994 ✓

Perfect! Matches expected output.

1 5
2 1
2 9999999994

Yes, exactly.

```python
import bisect

n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Precompute prefix sums: prefix[i] = sum of first i dormitories (0-indexed)
prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    prefix[i] = prefix[i - 1] + a[i - 1]

# Process each query
for query in b:
    # Find the leftmost index i such that prefix[i] >= query
    i = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, query)
    # Dormitory number is i (1-indexed meaning: i=1 means first dorm, i=2 means second dorm, etc.)
    dormitory = i
    # Room number in that dormitory
    room = query - prefix[i - 1]
    print(dormitory, room)
```

import bisect

n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Precompute prefix sums: prefix[i] = sum of first i dormitories (0-indexed)
prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    prefix[i] = prefix[i - 1] + a[i - 1]

# Process each query
for query in b:

    i = bisect.bisect_left(prefix, query)
    # Dormitory number is i (1-indexed meaning: i=1 means first dorm, i=2 means second dorm, etc.)
    dormitory = i

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The Fair Nut lives in $n$ story house. $a_i$ people live on the $i$-th floor of the house. Every person uses elevator twice a day: to get from the floor where he/she lives to the ground (first) floor and to get from the first floor to the floor where he/she lives, when he/she comes back home in the evening. 

It was decided that elevator, when it is not used, will stay on the $x$-th floor, but $x$ hasn't been chosen yet. When a person needs to get from floor $a$ to floor $b$, elevator follows the simple algorithm:   Moves from the $x$-th floor (initially it stays on the $x$-th floor) to the $a$-th and takes the passenger.  Moves from the $a$-th floor to the $b$-th floor and lets out the passenger (if $a$ equals $b$, elevator just opens and closes the doors, but still comes to the floor from the $x$-th floor).  Moves from the $b$-th floor back to the $x$-th.  The elevator never transposes more than one person and always goes back to the floor $x$ before transposing a next passenger. The elevator spends one unit of electricity to move between neighboring floors. So moving from the $a$-th floor to the $b$-th floor requires $|a - b|$ units of electricity.

Your task is to help Nut to find the minimum number of electricity units, that it would be enough for one day, by choosing an optimal the $x$-th floor. Don't forget than elevator initially stays on the $x$-th floor. 


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 100$) — the number of floors.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($0 \leq a_i \leq 100$) — the number of people on each floor.


-----Output-----

In a single line, print the answer to the problem — the minimum number of electricity units.


-----Examples-----
Input
3
0 2 1

Output
16
Input
2
1 1

Output
4


-----Note-----

In the first example, the answer can be achieved by choosing the second floor as the $x$-th floor. Each person from the second floor (there are two of them) would spend $4$ units of electricity per day ($2$ to get down and $2$ to get up), and one person from the third would spend $8$ units of electricity per day ($4$ to get down and $4$ to get up). $4 \cdot 2 + 8 \cdot 1 = 16$.

In the second example, the answer can be achieved by choosing the first floor as the $x$-th floor.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n-story house with `a_i` people on floor i
2. Each person uses the elevator twice daily:
   - From their floor to ground floor (1st floor) in the morning
   - From ground floor to their floor in the evening
3. The elevator starts at floor x and must return to floor x after each trip
4. Electricity cost is 1 unit per floor movement
5. We need to find the optimal floor x that minimizes total electricity consumption

## Analysis of One Trip

For a person on floor `i`:
- Morning: go from floor x to floor i, then from floor i to floor 1
- Evening: go from floor x to floor 1, then from floor 1 to floor i

Wait, let me re-read the problem: "when a person needs to get from floor a to floor b"

Actually, looking at the description more carefully:
- From floor a to floor b: elevator goes from x → a → b → x
- So the cost is |x - a| + |a - b| + |b - x|

For a person on floor i:
- Morning: from floor i to floor 1: cost = |x - i| + |i - 1| + |1 - x| = |x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|
- Evening: from floor 1 to floor i: cost = |x - 1| + |1 - i| + |i - x| = |x - 1| + |i - 1| + |i - x|

Wait, let me simplify this. Since |i - 1| = |1 - i| and |x - i| = |i - x|, both trips have the same cost structure.

For morning trip (i → 1):
- x → i → 1 → x
- Cost = |x - i| + |i - 1| + |1 - x| = |x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|

For evening trip (1 → i):
- x → 1 → i → x
- Cost = |x - 1| + |1 - i| + |i - x| = |x - 1| + |i - 1| + |x - i|

Since |x - i| = |i - x| and |x - 1| = |1 - x|, both trips have the same cost: |x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|

So total cost for one person on floor i = 2 * (|x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|)

Wait, let me verify with the example:
- Example: n=3, a=[0,2,1], answer=16
- If x=2 (second floor), then:
  - Floor 1: a_1 = 0 people, so no contribution
  - Floor 2: a_2 = 2 people
    - Each person: cost = 2 * (|2-2| + |2-1| + |2-1|) = 2 * (0 + 1 + 1) = 4
    - Total for 2 people: 2 * 4 = 8
  - Floor 3: a_3 = 1 person
    - cost = 2 * (|2-3| + |3-1| + |2-1|) = 2 * (1 + 2 + 1) = 8
    - Total for 1 person: 8
  - Grand total: 8 + 8 = 16 ✓

Perfect! So the formula is correct.

For a person on floor i (1-indexed), total daily cost = 2 * (|x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|)

But note: when i=1, floor 1 has people, and |i-1| = 0, so cost becomes 2 * (|x-1| + 0 + |x-1|) = 4 * |x-1|

For floor i > 1: cost = 2 * (|x-i| + (i-1) + |x-1|)

## Solution Approach

Since n ≤ 100 and a_i ≤ 100, we can try all possible values of x from 1 to n and compute the total electricity.

For each candidate x from 1 to n:
- For each floor i from 1 to n:
  - If a_i > 0, add 2 * (|x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|) to total cost
- Keep track of the minimum total cost

Wait, let me double-check the evening trip. Let me trace through the example more carefully:

For a person on floor i going to floor 1:
- Elevator goes from x to i (cost |x-i|)
- Then i to 1 (cost |i-1|)
- Then 1 to x (cost |1-x| = |x-1|)
- Total for one person: |x-i| + |i-1| + |x-1|

For a person on floor 1 going to floor 1 (same floor):
- Elevator goes from x to 1 (cost |x-1|)
- Then 1 to 1 (cost 0)
- Then 1 to x (cost |1-x| = |x-1|)
- Total: 2 * |x-1|

But in our formula, when i=1, we get: |x-1| + |1-1| + |x-1| = 2 * |x-1|, which matches.

Actually, looking back at the problem: "to get from the floor where he/she lives to the ground (first) floor" and "to get from the first floor to the floor where he/she lives".

So each person makes two trips:
1. From their floor to ground: cost = |x - i| + |i - 1| + |1 - x| = |x-i| + |i-1| + |x-1|
2. From ground to their floor: cost = |x - 1| + |1 - i| + |i - x| = |x-1| + |i-1| + |i-x| = |x-1| + |i-1| + |x-i|

So total cost per person = 2 * (|x-i| + |i-1| + |x-1|)

This confirms our formula.

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

min_electricity = float('inf')

# Try each possible x from 1 to n (1-indexed)
for x in range(1, n + 1):
    total_cost = 0
    # For each floor i (1-indexed, so i-1 in array)
    for i in range(n):
        floor_num = i + 1
        people = a[i]
        if people > 0:
            # Cost for one person on floor floor_num
            # Morning: x -> floor_num -> 1 -> x
            # Evening: x -> 1 -> floor_num -> x
            # Total per person: 2 * (|x - floor_num| + |floor_num - 1| + |x - 1|)
            cost_per_person = 2 * (abs(x - floor_num) + abs(floor_num - 1) + abs(x - 1))
            total_cost += people * cost_per_person
    
    min_electricity = min(min_electricity, total_cost)

print(min_electricity)
```

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=3, a=[0,2,1]
- x=1: 
  - floor 1: 0 people
  - floor 2: 2 people: cost_per_person = 2 * (|1-2| + |2-1| + |1-1|) = 2 * (1+1+0) = 4, total = 2*4 = 8
  - floor 3: 1 person: cost_per_person = 2 * (|1-3| + |3-1| + |1-1|) = 2 * (2+2+0) = 8, total = 8
  - grand total = 16
- x=2:
  - floor 1: 0
  - floor 2: 2: cost_per_person = 2 * (|2-2| + |2-1| + |2-1|) = 2 * (0+1+1) = 4, total = 8
  - floor 3: 1: cost_per_person = 2 * (|2-3| + |3-1| + |2-1|) = 2 * (1+2+1) = 8, total = 8
  - grand total = 16
- x=3:
  - floor 1: 0
  - floor 2: 2: cost_per_person = 2 * (|3-2| + |2-1| + |3-1|) = 2 * (1+1+2) = 8, total = 16
  - floor 3: 1: cost_per_person = 2 * (|3-3| + |3-1| + |3-1|) = 2 * (0+2+2) = 8, total = 8
  - grand total = 24

So minimum is 16. ✓

Example 2: n=2, a=[1,1]
- x=1:
  - floor 1: 1 person: cost_per_person = 2 * (|1-1| + |1-1| + |1-1|) = 0
  - floor 2: 1 person: cost_per_person = 2 * (|1-2| + |2-1| + |1-1|) = 2 * (1+1+0) = 4
  - total = 4
- x=2:
  - floor 1: 1 person: cost_per_person = 2 * (|2-1| + |1-1| + |2-1|) = 2 * (1+0+1) = 4
  - floor 2: 1 person: cost_per_person = 2 * (|2-2| + |2-1| + |2-1|) = 2 * (0+1+1) = 4
  - total = 8

Minimum is 4. ✓

Perfect!

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n-story house with `a_i` people on floor i
2. Each person uses the elevator twice daily:
   - Once to go from their floor to ground floor (floor 1)
   - Once to go from ground floor to their floor in the evening
3. The elevator starts at floor x and must return to floor x after each trip
4. The electricity cost for moving from floor a to floor b is |a - b|
5. We need to find the optimal floor x that minimizes total electricity consumption

## Step-by-step Analysis

Let me trace through what happens for a person on floor i:

1. **Going down from floor i to floor 1:**
   - Elevator moves from x to i: cost = |x - i|
   - Elevator moves from i to 1: cost = |i - 1|
   - Total cost for downward trip = |x - i| + |i - 1|

2. **Going up from floor 1 to floor i:**
   - Elevator moves from x to 1: cost = |x - 1|
   - Elevator moves from 1 to i: cost = |1 - i| = |i - 1|
   - Total cost for upward trip = |x - 1| + |i - 1|

3. **Total cost for one person on floor i:**
   - Total = (|x - i| + |i - 1|) + (|x - 1| + |i - 1|)
   - Total = |x - i| + |x - 1| + 2|i - 1|

4. **Total cost for all people:**
   - Sum over all floors i from 1 to n: a_i * (|x - i| + |x - 1| + 2|i - 1|)

Since we have people on each floor i with count a_i, the total cost becomes:
- Sum over i=1 to n: a_i * (|x - i| + |x - 1| + 2|i - 1|)

We can break this down:
- Sum over i: a_i * |x - i| + a_i * |x - 1| + 2 * a_i * |i - 1|

Note that |x - 1| is constant for a given x, so:
- a_i * |x - 1| term: this is constant for all i, so it's (|x - 1|) * (sum of all a_i)

Actually, let me be more careful: a_i * |x - 1| means for each floor i, we multiply |x - 1| by a_i, so it's |x - 1| * (sum of all a_i)

So the total cost is:
- Σ(a_i * |x - i|) + |x - 1| * Σ(a_i) + 2 * Σ(a_i * |i - 1|)

The last term (2 * Σ(a_i * |i - 1|)) is constant for any x, since it doesn't depend on x.

Therefore, the total cost depends on x only through:
- Σ(a_i * |x - i|) + |x - 1| * Σ(a_i)

So we can compute the total cost for each possible x (from 1 to n) and find the minimum.

## Algorithm

1. Read input: n and array a of people on each floor
2. For each possible x from 1 to n:
   - Calculate total_cost = 0
   - For each floor i from 1 to n:
     - Add a_i * (|x - i| + |x - 1| + 2 * |i - 1|)
   - Keep track of minimum total_cost
3. Return minimum total_cost

Let me verify with the examples:

**Example 1:** n=3, a=[0,2,1]
- Floor 1: a_1=0, people=0
- Floor 2: a_2=2, people=2  
- Floor 3: a_3=1, people=1

Try x=1:
- For floor 1: 0 * (|1-1| + |1-1| + 2*|1-1|) = 0
- For floor 2: 2 * (|1-2| + |1-1| + 2*|2-1|) = 2 * (1 + 0 + 2*1) = 2 * 3 = 6
- For floor 3: 1 * (|1-3| + |1-1| + 2*|3-1|) = 1 * (2 + 0 + 2*2) = 1 * 6 = 6
- Total = 0 + 6 + 6 = 12

Wait, but expected output is 16. Let me check the example explanation.

Example says: "each person from the second floor (there are two of them) would spend 4 units of electricity per day (2 to get down and 2 to get up), and one person from the third would spend 8 units of electricity per day (4 to get down and 4 to get up). 4 * 2 + 8 * 1 = 16"

Let me recalculate manually for x=2:
- For floor 2: person goes down: |2-2| + |2-1| = 0 + 1 = 1, then up: |2-1| + |1-2| = 1 + 1 = 2, so total 3? 
Wait, let me re-read the problem.

"Moves from the x-th floor (initially it stays on the x-th floor) to the a-th and takes the passenger. Moves from the a-th floor to the b-th floor and lets out the passenger (if a equals b, elevator just opens and closes the doors, but still comes to the floor from the x-th floor). Moves from the b-th floor back to the x-th."

So for going down from floor i to floor 1:
1. x → i: cost = |x - i|
2. i → 1: cost = |i - 1|
3. 1 → x: cost = |1 - x| = |x - 1|

Wait, that's different! The elevator goes from b back to x, not to 1.

Let me correct:

**Going down from floor i to floor 1:**
- x → i: cost = |x - i|
- i → 1: cost = |i - 1|
- 1 → x: cost = |1 - x| = |x - 1|
- Total = |x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|

**Going up from floor 1 to floor i:**
- x → 1: cost = |x - 1|
- 1 → i: cost = |i - 1|
- i → x: cost = |i - x| = |x - i|
- Total = |x - 1| + |i - 1| + |x - i|

**Total for one person on floor i:**
- (|x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|) + (|x - 1| + |i - 1| + |x - i|)
- = 2|x - i| + 2|i - 1| + 2|x - 1|

Wait, this can't be right because both terms have |x - i|. Let me check:

Downward: |x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|
Upward: |x - 1| + |i - 1| + |x - i|

Adding: |x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1| + |x - 1| + |i - 1| + |x - i| = 2|x - i| + 2|i - 1| + 2|x - 1|

But this would mean each person on floor i uses 2(|x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|) electricity.

Let me verify with the example:
x=2, i=2 (floor 2 with 2 people):
- |2-2| + |2-1| + |2-1| = 0 + 1 + 1 = 2 for downward part
- For upward: |2-1| + |2-1| + |2-2| = 1 + 1 + 0 = 2
- Total per person: 2 + 2 = 4 ✓

For i=3 (floor 3, 1 person):
- |2-3| + |3-1| + |2-1| = 1 + 2 + 1 = 4 for downward
- Upward: |2-1| + |3-1| + |2-3| = 1 + 2 + 1 = 4
- Total per person: 4 + 4 = 8 ✓

Total: 2*4 + 1*8 = 8 + 8 = 16 ✓

So the formula for one person on floor i is: 2(|x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|)

Total cost = Σ(a_i * 2(|x - i| + |i - 1| + |x - 1|)) = 2 * [Σ(a_i * |x - i|) + Σ(a_i * |i - 1|) + Σ(a_i * |x - 1|)]

Since |x - 1| is constant for a fixed x, Σ(a_i * |x - 1|) = |x - 1| * Σ(a_i)

So total cost = 2 * [Σ(a_i * |x - i|) + |x - 1| * Σ(a_i) + Σ(a_i * |i - 1|)]

And Σ(a_i * |i - 1|) is constant for any x, so we can precompute it.

Therefore, we can compute:
- total_cost(x) = 2 * (Σ(a_i * |x - i|) + |x - 1| * total_people + constant)

Where constant = Σ(a_i * |i - 1|)

We can compute total_people = Σ(a_i) and constant = Σ(a_i * |i - 1|)

Then for each x from 1 to n, compute the sum of a_i * |x - i|, add to (|x - 1| * total_people) + constant, multiply by 2.

Let me implement this.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed: a[0] is floor 1, a[1] is floor 2, etc.
# So floor i corresponds to index i-1

total_people = sum(a)
constant = 0
for i in range(n):
    floor_i = i + 1  # floor number
    constant += a[i] * abs(floor_i - 1)  # |i - 1| where i is floor number

min_cost = float('inf')

for x in range(1, n + 1):  # x from 1 to n
    cost_sum = 0
    for i in range(n):
        floor_i = i + 1
        cost_sum += a[i] * abs(x - floor_i)
    
    # Total cost with the formula
    total_cost = 2 * (cost_sum + abs(x - 1) * total_people + constant)
    min_cost = min(min_cost, total_cost)

print(min_cost)
```

Let me verify with the second example:

Input: 2, [1, 1]
- n=2, a=[1,1]
- total_people = 1+1 = 2
- constant = 1*|1-1| + 1*|2-1| = 1*0 + 1*1 = 1

Try x=1:
- cost_sum = 1*|1-1| + 1*|1-2| = 1*0 + 1*1 = 1
- total_cost = 2 * (1 + |1-1|*2 + 1) = 2 * (1 + 0 + 1) = 2 * 2 = 4 ✓

Try x=2:
- cost_sum = 1*|2-1| + 1*|2-2| = 1*1 + 1*0 = 1
- total_cost = 2 * (1 + |2-1|*2 + 1) = 2 * (1 + 2 + 1) = 2 * 4 = 8

Minimum is 4 ✓

This matches the expected output.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The Fair Nut lives in $n$ story house. $a_i$ people live on the $i$-th floor of the house. Every person uses elevator twice a day: to get from the floor where he/she lives to the ground (first) floor and to get from the first floor to the floor where he/she lives, when he/she comes back home in the evening. 

It was decided that elevator, when it is not used, will stay on the $x$-th floor, but $x$ hasn't been chosen yet. When a person needs to get from floor $a$ to floor $b$, elevator follows the simple algorithm:   Moves from the $x$-th floor (initially it stays on the $x$-th floor) to the $a$-th and takes the passenger.  Moves from the $a$-th floor to the $b$-th floor and lets out the passenger (if $a$ equals $b$, elevator just opens and closes the doors, but still comes to the floor from the $x$-th floor).  Moves from the $b$-th floor back to the $x$-th.  The elevator never transposes more than one person and always goes back to the floor $x$ before transposing a next passenger. The elevator spends one unit of electricity to move between neighboring floors. So moving from the $a$-th floor to the $b$-th floor requires $|a - b|$ units of electricity.

Your task is to help Nut to find the minimum number of electricity units, that it would be enough for one day, by choosing an optimal the $x$-th floor. Don't forget than elevator initially stays on the $x$-th floor. 


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 100$) — the number of floors.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($0 \leq a_i \leq 100$) — the number of people on each floor.


-----Output-----

In a single line, print the answer to the problem — the minimum number of electricity units.


-----Examples-----
Input
3
0 2 1

Output
16
Input
2
1 1

Output
4


-----Note-----

In the first example, the answer can be achieved by choosing the second floor as the $x$-th floor. Each person from the second floor (there are two of them) would spend $4$ units of electricity per day ($2$ to get down and $2$ to get up), and one person from the third would spend $8$ units of electricity per day ($4$ to get down and $4$ to get up). $4 \cdot 2 + 8 \cdot 1 = 16$.

In the second example, the answer can be achieved by choosing the first floor as the $x$-th floor.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout
